ParlaMint-DK_20170323100444	Does anyone wish to speak? Here you go, sir. Pelle Dragsted from Enhedslisten. It'd be really good if you just remember to get in. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170323100520	I'm sorry, President. Thank you. This proposal is, after all, about the pay and pension conditions of ministers. In the Enhedslisten, we have been working for many years to normalise the pension situation of politicians, so that our relationship with the rest of the population is more abated. It is therefore also positive with this bill that a few small steps are being taken that limit the most unbearable privileges that we are covered by. Unfortunately, the draft law is quite inadequate and, in fact, runs the risk of making politicians more advantageous in certain areas than with the rules in force. That is why we have tabled a number of amendments from the Enhedslisten which we hope will be supported. Unfortunately, when it comes to the retirement of ministers, the parties behind the agreement have chosen not to follow the remuneration commission's proposal for a contribution-based pension, which is more like the occupational pensions to which other employees are subject. Instead, this very lucrative civil servant-like pension scheme has been maintained, where politicians can earn a very high pension much faster than virtually all other Danes. But that can be corrected now, because we have tabled an amendment from the Enhedslisten, which means that we are giving up the privilege pension for ministers and, instead, following the recommendation of the Pay Commission to move to a savings-based pension, which is more like the occupational pension to which other Danes are subject. Then there is the question of retirement age. This proposal has raised the retirement age for ministers, so that in future it will follow the old age of retirement. As has been suggested, this little trick has been introduced to introduce something called an early retirement pension. And this is not the kind of early retirement that we know from other Danes, with work tests and medical declarations and bureaucracy and things like that. No, this is an early retirement pension which is completely independent of health and which you can just choose if you want and which, combined with the very lucrative pension, means that in future ministers can also retire with a high pension 5 years before the retirement age and can even receive the pension, while they have other high-income jobs. We don't think that's okay. The right to early retirement, despite the fact that a deduction is made, will nevertheless mean that it can be economically lucrative to opt for early retirement in relation to waiting to retire, since, depending on the overall life cycle, a higher amount of pension can be paid than if one waits for the retirement age, i.e. an improvement in the current rules. That problem will also be solved if our amendment on a savings-based pension is adopted. Alternatively, during the third reading, we will propose that the possibility of early retirement be completely removed. Finally, it is a major problem in relation to the pension that the change in pension rules only applies in the future and thus does not cover the part of the pension already earned by current and former ministers. This means, of course, that the ministers who took the lead some years ago in raising the retirement age of the population will themselves be able to raise their pensions many years before the retirement age, and I think that is a very unfortunate signal to send out. It should be the case that when we adopt rules that apply to the population, they must also apply to the politicians who adopt the rules. It has then been argued that it would be contrary to the basic law to include the pension already saved in the new rules, but the legal notes we have received are not so sloppy in their conclusions. There is talk that it may be; there is talk that it depends on where you set the limit and how long you let it go back. That is why we have tabled an amendment which means that the law will be revised in two years' time and that, in the meantime, we will have an independent legal assessment of the possibilities of allowing the generation of politicians who raised the retirement age of the population to be included in the new rules so that they themselves cannot retire as 60-year-olds or 62-year-olds as they can with the rules in force. A final and quite wrong signal from the proposal is that the 5% reduction in ministerial salaries that was adopted in the context of the recovery package in 2001 should be dropped, so that ministers' salaries are now increased. After all, that reduction was adopted at the time in 2001 because it was said that now that everyone else had to keep up with loss of benefit, it would be preferable to stick to it as a politician. So it's just a little funny that it's not like the attacks you made on ordinary people's relationships, for example. the shorter benefit period and a number of the other impairments in the recovery package have been removed again. But the deterioration imposed on ministers seems to us to have to go away now, and that is once again a strange signal to send out. So we have also tabled an amendment on the part of the Enhedslisten, which maintains the current salary situation of the ministers and, moreover, also removes the possibility of receiving a ministerial pension, while at the same time receiving remuneration or remuneration for certain other political duties. I hope, of course, that the majority will support these amendments, but at the same time I must say that this is not something I am counting on, because unfortunately we are experiencing an incarnate reluctance of a majority in this Parliament to do something about these privileges for politicians.

If I choose it as a minister or a member of parliament, the deduction is 6%. I think the questioner can see the difference.

Then it would have been a similar principle, for then you would have spent your own money. But that is not the case here. Here you get a deduction, and that deduction is just much, much less than what ordinary employees get if they choose to retire earlier. You can't see this separately from the extent of the pension saved as a member of parliament or as a minister. As members of parliament, we save in eight years the same thing as it might take a metal worker 20 years to save. ParlaMint-DK_20170323101410	I just think it is appropriate, at second reading, to thank first and foremost for the cooperation that has taken place in a broad circle of parties, to do this modernisation of the conditions for the remuneration and pensions of ministers. Then, in relation to our ministers, there is the possibility of stopping an earlier deduction corresponding to the pension scheme we have, namely the civil servant pension. I think it has been important for us to get this done so that the retirement age that parliamentary politicians and ministers have brought forward will fully follow the retirement age limits that apply to the rest of the population. I think that is essential. Then there is the whole discussion as to whether our remuneration and pensions should be different, in other words whether our pension scheme, rather than following the civil servant pension, as it has done historically, and as the proposal here should also continue to do, should not have followed an occupational pension. That is part of the Commission's proposal. But where the Salaries Commission said that they are proposing it at the same time and as part of a package that then means setting up the current remuneration to change the pension earnings that exist, Mr. Pelle Dragsted simply to take one side of the coin with him, and I have a vertical disagreement with that. I believe that we should neither be embarrassed nor ashamed of the salary we pay to our MPs or ministers in the Danish Parliament. It is at the level of what is in similar parliaments around, it is at the level of choosing not a political deed for money's sake, but also being paid for the work you do. That is why I think it is excellent that we are now getting these things straightened out and put in place, and I am glad that there is a broad majority rejecting the Enhedslisten's proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20170323102500	Sir. Pelle Dragsted would like to speak. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170323102521	Thank you. The proposal we are now debating is then about the pension situation of members of parliament. And, as I have said, this is also an area which we in the Enhedslist have been working for many years to normalise, in other words these pension conditions, so that they are more refugees with the rest of the population. Basically, we do not think that, as members of parliament, we should have excessive special privileges. The proposal, like that on ministers, contains some small progress, but not at all enough to deal with some of the very far-reaching privileges that politicians, including members of parliament, enjoy. Yes, I would like to say again that in some areas it is in fact in danger of increasing privileges. That is why we, the Enhedslisten, have tabled a number of amendments to this proposal which, if adopted, will help to create more reasonable conditions which are more similar to those of other Danes. Firstly, like what we have just discussed, the draft law means that in future politicians will also have a very lucrative pension scheme, where, in just a few years, much faster than virtually all other Danes, the right to a very high pension is acquired. And here, unfortunately, the majority have chosen to ignore the remuneration commission's proposal to move to a more moderate savings-based pension, which is more similar to the labour market pension of ordinary Danes. But again I would say that the Folketing now has a chance to get it right by voting in favour of the amendment tabled by Enhedslisten, which means that we follow the remuneration committee's recommendation for a pension based on savings. Secondly, the draft law means that the retirement age will be raised to the age of retirement, but, as in the case of ministers, this new early retirement has therefore been introduced. And again, it is not an early retirement scheme like the one we know from ordinary people, where you need to be checked and through various wear and tearing processes. No, it is a self-chosen system for which you can just choose if you want to, and even get paid even if you have a job next door. Combined with the very lucrative pension, it will mean that members of parliament can also retire in the future with a high pension 5 years before the retirement age and can even receive the pension, while working and having a high income next to it. And the right to this so-called early retirement will mean that, despite the deduction resulting from opting for early retirement, it can actually be economically more lucrative to go earlier, i.e. to get your pension paid earlier • thus more lucrative than to get it later but also more lucrative than today • because, depending on your life span, you can receive a higher amount of pension than if you wait until the retirement age. Finally, as I said, it is a major problem that the pension rules only apply in the future and thus do not cover the part of the pension already earned by members of parliament. In fact, this means that the generation of politicians in here who, a few years ago, led to an increase in the retirement age of the population will be able to raise their pension even many years before the age of retirement. It's not okay. The rules adopted for the people must also apply to the politicians who adopt them. That is why we have also tabled an amendment which means that we will obtain an independent legal assessment of the possibilities of including these changes in the pension that has already been earned. Let me turn now to some of the amendments tabled by the Socialist Group. After all, the Social Democrats have tabled an amendment to the effect that members of parliament should be able to choose for themselves whether they want to retain their very lucrative pension or whether they want to go down on an ordinary labour market-like pension such as the one that more flees with what ordinary people have. Let me be clear: I think this is a completely embarrassing proposal. Instead of listening to people's criticism of politicians' golden pension relationships; instead of following the remuneration commission's suggestion that politicians should go to an occupational pension-type scheme, you make this suggestion that those who are disgruntled, as they say, with this high level, can just go down to a lower pension themselves. Dear Social Democrats, that is not what this is all about. It's not about whether some people in here are unhappy with a high pension. It is about the Danes being dissatisfied because they rightly do not understand why politicians should have such far-reaching pension privileges, which means that you can actually retire with a golden pension long before ordinary Danes. And the discontent and politicianism that this creates does not disappear because some of us voluntarily retire while the rest of you hold onto the privileges. Speaking of sticking to the privileges: both the Social Democratic Party and the Danish People's Party have indicated that they will support this proposal, but I have not heard a single politician from the two parties indicate that they will take advantage of the new opportunity and renounce the golden pension, but of course I am looking forward to keeping up with that and seeing if you intend to do so. In the Enhedslisten, however, we have decided to support the Social Democratic proposal that in future we can voluntarily renounce the privilege pension and move on to a more general occupational pension, and we have also jointly decided that all our members of the Folketing will move on to the lower pension so that we get more on the same footing with other Danes. We have done so in a way because we are not afraid to let action follow words. In fact, we have been serious when we have been criticising these very privileged retirement conditions for years. It cannot be the case that politicians adopt deteriorations that affect all other ill, unemployed, worn-out conditions while maintaining extremely lucrative conditions for ourselves. We have also chosen to support the proposal and renounce the privileges, because we hope that by taking the lead we will be able to put new pressure on our fellow Members in here to put an end to the unfair pension conditions that many will still be covered by. It is therefore also, of course, absolutely essential for the Enhedslisten that, if there is a majority in favour of this, there must be full openness and transparency about which parties and politicians maintain the old privileged pension and who voluntarily gives it, for example. is shown on the individual politician's website in the Danish Parliament, so the Danes have the opportunity to follow who really believes that they should have pension conditions that lie miles away from what ordinary Danes have. The Enhedslisten will table an amendment on this at the third reading, and of course I expect the Social Democrats to support it. Because I can read from the explanatory statement of the proposal that you are fully committed to the pension situation that you have. So I expect there to be agreement on that, but it may be that the Socialist rapporteur can go up and confirm it. That was the speech I was going to make, but just before I went to the platform here, a news was rolled into the Ekstra Bladet, and some quite surprising news has come out this morning just before the debate, namely that the Social Democrats' amendments have a very unfortunate consequence and a derivative effect. For as the proposal stands now, it will actually mean that politicians who have been in parliament for more than 20 years will be able to have even greater pension privileges than today. It is the case that, when you have been sitting here for 20 years, you have earned the maximum pension under the current system, but if the Social Democrats' proposal is adopted as it stands, people who have then reached this ceiling, Members who have been sitting here for more than 20 years, or ministers who have been sitting for shorter periods, will be able to choose to jump onto the labour market pension and then add to it, and that is, of course, totally unacceptable. The aim was, of course, to remove privileges and not extend them, and I would like to commend Mr SF. Jacob Mark for discovering this very serious hole. I haven't been smart enough for that myself. But with this new information, we cannot vote in favour of the amendment as it stands today, and I would therefore propose that the draft law be referred back to committee, and I would urge the Social Democrats to table a new amendment closing this gap, so as to maintain the proposal that has been put forward but without this opportunity to add. For even if Mr. Henrik Sass Larsen is a clever fox, I don't think it has been intended that he should be able to add to his already saved pension. So that will be the request from this and we will, of course, vote for the new amendment on behalf of the Enhedslisten. To conclude, I would just like to say that we have been working for many years to normalise the pension situation of politicians, and that is not because it is about a great deal of money. When we insist on holding on to unfair privileges, while ordinary Danes are hit by reforms and higher retirement age, we basically send a signal saying: We are worth more than you. And we in the Enhedslist will not be able to send that signal. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20170323104118	The debate is open, and that is Mr. Thomas Jensen, Social Democrat, as rapporteur. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170323104147	Thank you, President. The financial crisis showed us that the regulation of the financial markets was not good enough and fine-meshed enough. Deregulation from the 1980s and up to the 00s meant that unscrupulous speculators could start the financial crisis, which then developed into a real economic and social crisis with unemployment, where ordinary employees lost their pensions and life savings. Now, nine years after some of the world's economies are getting back into business, let us not forget that it is our responsibility as legislators to prevent a repetition of the financial crisis. Already in 2009, the G20 Summit in London decided to strengthen the financial system by developing financial regulation and building a more delicate network for the supervision of capital markets. Since then, we in the Danish Parliament have taken many different steps to ensure financial stability. And the two legislative proposals we are working together today are an update, an extension and an adaptation of this regulation to Danish legislation. Overall, legislative proposal L 155 has to address the problem that a wide range of transactions in financial instruments are currently taking place outside the trading venues currently regulated by MiFID I from 2004. Trades have been more automated than in the past and a number of new market players have been added. Here, then, we must make sure that trade takes place in a regulated market where there are clear rules within which players must remain. A number of new areas are therefore also subject to regulation, and it is most important to emphasise that there is a sharper focus on such more computer-controlled trades, which are, after all, developing strongly. These are all steps in the right direction. There are many elements in the two legislative proposals, and I will not go through them all, because there is absolutely no time for that here, even though the speaking time here at first reading has been extended. But I will try to crack down on some of those that I think are the most important to work hard on in committee. In L 155 there is this section on algorithmic trade, and in an algorithmic trade a computer makes a large number of trades based on pre-programmed instructions. Applicable Danish law today does not contain special rules on the use of algorithmic trade, and this bill must then be corrected. What is in the draft law is the introduction of a number of new rules designed to reduce the risk of an algorithmic trade causing market disruption. This also applies to algorithmically high-frequency trading, which is a special form of algorithmic trading, where a trading system analyses market movements in a very short time and then responds to the analysis by placing a large number of orders. What is the content here is that the proposal requires fund brokers using algorithmic trading to have control measures to ensure that trades do not disrupt the market. And fund-brokers offering their customers direct electronic access to the markets through their trading facilities must carry out continuous checks on customers' suitability and use of access. Then it is also the case that there has to be some control. Fund brokers must inform the FSA of their application and strategies for algorithmic trading and testing of their systems. Furthermore, it is required that trading venues where algorithmic trading is used have the necessary systems and control measures to deal with severe periods of peak and market stress. In the extreme, the draft law means that trading venues must be able to suspend and restrict trade in large price fluctuations caused by the use of algorithmic trading. It is good and positive that regulation of computer-based algorithmic trades is now being introduced at all, but during committee reading and during a thorough technical review of this bill, it would be appropriate for us to have a slightly better overview of whether this bill, the new regulation, is sufficient to ensure that algorithmic trades do not disrupt the market and help to start a new financial crisis. Then there's something about the management of these companies and the management of regulated markets. I have to say that the stories of the financial crisis with more or less incompetent directors continue to be frightening. It is therefore also a good thing that directors or holders should at all times be suitable for the task, be honest and, of course, devote sufficient time to their duties or duties. The proposal also limits the number of management posts that a management member can hold in other companies; demands are made on the composition of the board, the overall competences of the board; and calls for the allocation of resources for introductory courses and further training of board members. In fact, competent management is needed, and it is my view that the proposed rules are essential steps in the right direction. That is good and it is positive. With regard to the supervisory and investigative powers of the FSA, it is proposed that it is the FSA that is designated as the competent authority to ensure compliance with the law, and then in the hearing answers some people believe that supervision and inspection should not be carried out without a court order. Here it is essential to stress that the Danish Financial Supervisory Authority and also the Danish Business Authority are subject to the principle of proportionality, where they first have to obtain information before an inspection is carried out, and if there is a suspicion of a criminal offence against the law, the case has to be handed over to SØIK, which has to carry out a possible search in accordance with the rules of the Code of Procedure. So all in all, I consider that legal certainty is quite well balanced in the legislative proposal, and it is good that it is proposed that two years after the entry into force of the law it should be evaluated whether these supervisory powers are also applied well enough. Then there is the whole level of sanctions if the law is violated. It is the case that the level of fines for breaches of the capital market law is increased by the bill, and that is a good thing. For natural persons, the fine level is from 10,000 DKK. and up to a net monthly salary and, for legal persons, the net annual turnover shall be taken into account at the time of the offence. These are all steps in the right direction. Then there is the law on the resale right L 156, which adapts the law on financial activities and the law on financial advisors to the directives and regulations to be implemented through L 155, and there is one particular point that comes to my mind, namely the remuneration of employees selling or advising on financial instruments. In fact, it is proposed that companies should ensure that the remuneration of the company's employees is not contrary to the undertaking's obligation to act in the best interests of customers, including in accordance with the customary rules. And a ban is proposed on the variable remuneration of employees working with sales targets who could, at worst, encourage action against the best interests of the retail customer. It sounds good to have a healthier pay policy, but in committee I want to make sure that there are no corners of the financial sector where there may be unhealthy pay opportunities left. Then there are a large number of areas in the legislative proposals that give rise to committee issues. That is, for example, the case. What matters is that prospectuses below 5 million. The euro does not need to be approved and there is something missing about regulation in takeover bids, and then there is something in the proposal for cross-border stock exchange activity as well as something about the so-called tied agents, where I think there is a need for clarification as to why the draft law is drafted as it is. Then there is also what we have spent a great deal of time on in the Parliament's Committee on Economic and Monetary Affairs and here in the Parliamentary Assembly: the whole question of mortgage credit institutions, in other words, what the consequences for our mortgage credit institutions are with this bill. It is a question here of capital requirements, of the relationship between the internal models of mortgage credit institutions and the standard models that they should be able to follow, and of the impact this will have on individual borrowers in relation to the development of contribution rates. It is also something that we should try to get a little bit of drilling out during the committee procedure. Finally, I would like to say that the more than 1,000 pages of legislative proposals that we have had the opportunity to study since the presentation eight days ago are a great mouthful. I think it is for all of us. It may well happen that I underestimate my fellow rapporteurs in this Chamber today, but I think that we all have areas in these two legislative proposals where, when we have the objective of adopting good legislation here in the Danish Parliament, it might be appropriate for us to have a good and thorough technical review in the Committee on Industry. I also think it would have been useful if it had been something that had happened per year. Automatically, before we had to stand here and deal first with the bill today. But now we are standing here and let us take a good technical review between the first and second readings. Overall, I can say that the Social Democratic Party supports the measures to implement the directive and regulation into Danish law, but I must stress that there may be areas where we gain insight into some aspects of the legislative proposal which should be better regulated than what has been proposed here today, and therefore I look forward to a very good and thorough committee reading.

So, of course, I think it is a very good idea for us to have a technical review in committee, as the Socialist rapporteur, Mr. Thomas Jensen, first proposed, and as several rapporteurs have also followed up. So we need to have a dialogue on this, both during the technical review but also during the examination of the proposal. And there must be enough time for us to have a dialogue on how to make the best of these legislative proposals, which, of course, should make our capital markets and financial institutions and the whole area stronger and more robust for the world we live in. Because the point is that with these legislative proposals we are modernising and we are radically tightening up the entire market for securities, etc. and the layout of marketplaces. It is, after all, a major legislative proposal, or both legislative proposals, and they represent the most important regulatory initiative in the field of capital markets since the introduction of stock exchange reform II in 1995 which formed the basis for the preparation of the Securities Trading Act. The aim of the two legislative proposals is to ensure efficient and efficient capital markets, and this is of course to the benefit of financial stability and of the ability of ordinary companies to raise capital for investment and growth purposes. As a society, we also have a particular interest in the proper and appropriate functioning of the capital markets so that they are trusted. The legislative proposals therefore also increase investor protection and strengthen transparency in order to ensure that confidence in capital markets is maintained. This is demonstrated by the fact that in future a number of actors will be regulated and supervised. This is true, for example. data reporting services providers. The providers handle and publish key information about the volume and price of the trade, etc., which investors make decisions on the basis of. Similarly, the information is used to monitor trades and prevent price manipulation. Those actors therefore play a key role as intermediaries of information when securities etc. are traded, and therefore it is important that there is a proper framework within which they can act. It is essential for there to be confidence in the providers and their services. Furthermore, we ensure that all organised trading in financial securities etc. in future trading venues will be regulated, helping to ensure transparency for investors. Trading venues play a crucial role in the capital market by virtue of their role as intermediaries of contacts between issuers of securities and investors. That is why it is important for there to be adequate security and confidence in this. In addition, the legislative proposals take account of the increasing technological developments that have taken place over the years. That is why the legislative proposals include: ensured a sharper focus on computer-controlled trading, also called high-frequency trading. This should reduce the risk of computer-controlled transactions causing disruption on the capital markets. Overall, the proposals aim to ensure greater transparency on the capital markets to the benefit of investors' confidence in these markets and stronger regulation of market players in order to maintain proper and well-functioning markets and to strengthen investor protection. I look forward to a good discussion of the legislative proposals during committee reading. As I said, we will ensure that there is a technical review of the proposals, and I am very constructive in relation to the dialogue and discussion that will take place on the basis of the technical review.

ParlaMint-DK_20170323114358	Thank you. Then there are no more brief comments. Then it's mr. Martin Henriksen, Danish People's Party. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170323114404	Thank you. The aim of the bill is to translate the part of the Finance Act which deals with tighter rules for permanent stay in Denmark, also called unlimited stay. And now I can tell you that the Danish People's Party supports the bill. The residence requirement is tightened so that the requirement for a number of years of legal residence in Denmark is increased from 6 years to 8 years. The change is also a tightening of the family reunification rules, as it has an impact on access to spouse reunification, since today immigrants are usually required to have a permanent residence permit for three years in order to be able to be brought together with a spouse. So we are really tightening both the rules on permanent residence and the rules on family reunification. We are also tightening up the self-reliance requirement and the employment requirement. The requirement for self-employment is tightened so that foreigners may not have received public support during the four years preceding the application. Today the requirement is 3 years. So here's a one-year sharpening. The employment requirement will be tightened to require a foreigner to have been in full-time employment for at least 3 years and 6 months in the last 4 years in order to obtain a permanent residence permit. Today the requirement is 2 years and 6 months in the last 3 years. In addition, we make it more difficult to obtain permanent residence permits if you have committed crime. This is also a reasonable and absolutely necessary tightening up from the point of view of the Danish People's Party, and it is also something that we have stressed in the discussions that have taken place in connection with the financial law negotiations, and also in the negotiations that were before it. It is worth noting that today there is a requirement to obtain a permanent residence permit that the applicant fulfils at least two out of four additional integration-relevant conditions, as it so finely states. We'll keep that part. If all four additional conditions are met, the alien can obtain an unlimited residence permit after four years. It is a special scheme which already exists today and the scheme is being continued. The bill doesn't really affect that part. Overall, this is a significant tightening up compared with today. Parliament has already tightened the rules, as has also been mentioned by a few rapporteurs, once since the parliamentary elections, and now we are doing it again. The number of permanent residents has fallen since the parliamentary elections, and it is the Danish People's Party's hope that with this new tightening the number will decrease further. If the bill is adopted, it will also be more difficult to obtain Danish citizenship, as it is a requirement that the applicant has a permanent residence permit, i.e. an unlimited stay in Denmark before a Danish passport can be granted here in Parliament. In this way, we are actually tightening up the requirements of those who wish to become Danish citizens. So it is a tightening-up for permanent residence permits, it is a tightening-up of family reunification rules, and so it is also an indirect tightening of the rules of citizenship. The Danish People's Party asked for this tightening up to be included in the financial law agreement, which is why we can, of course, support the bill, as we see it as an improvement in existing legislation.

ParlaMint-DK_20170323131351	Thank you for your contributions. I can of course note that there seems to be relatively broad support for the legislative proposal here. As has already been said, the aim of the draft law is to translate the initiative on tighter rules of residence in Denmark into the Financial Law Agreement for 2017. The possibility of obtaining permanent residence permits in Denmark is, in the view of the government, a privilege, and the parties to the agreement agree that higher demands must be made on the ability and will of refugees and immigrants, not least to integrate into Danish society. It is therefore proposed to tighten up a number of the basic conditions for obtaining permanent residence permits. Firstly, the residence requirement is tightened and this means that the requirement for the number of years to have been legally resident in Denmark is increased from 6 to 8 years. This has a secondary effect on spouse reunification, because immigrants are normally required to have a permanent residence permit for three years in order to have a spouse reunification. Immigrants must also, as a rule, have had 11 years of legal residence in order to obtain spouse reunification. As the draft law also shows, we are on the verge of what is possible under Article 8 of the European Convention on Human Rights. You may disagree with that, but my view is that it is a process risk that we have to run as the world now looks. Secondly, the requirement of good repute is being tightened up, i.e. the rules that, if you have committed more serious crimes, you will be completely excluded from obtaining permanent residence permits. Today, foreigners who have been sentenced to 1 year's unconditional imprisonment or more are excluded from obtaining permanent residence permits. From now on, this is already happening with a six-month sentence. Thirdly, the seasons are being tightened up, i.e.: The rules on the exclusion of permanent residence permits for a period of time, where crime has been committed, and with the tightening up of the waiting periods, in order to obtain permanent residence permits to a greater extent, correspond to the periods of citizenship, i.e. citizenship. This means, among other things, that the waiting period for unconditional imprisonment is extended from 41⁄2 to 6 years and that the waiting period for unconditional imprisonment of between 60 days and 6 months is extended from today 12 years to 15 years. Fourthly, the requirement for self-reliance is tightened. Today, foreigners may not have received certain types of public support during the last three years prior to the application for a permanent residence permit, and it is now raised to four years. Finally, fifthly, the employment requirement is being tightened up. Today, a foreigner is required to have been in ordinary full-time employment or to have pursued a self-employed activity for at least 21⁄2 years in the last 3 years in order to obtain a permanent residence permit. The employment requirement is now raised to 31⁄2 years in the last 4 years. A number of the basic conditions for permanent residence permit remain unchanged. This is, for example, the case. on the requirement that foreigners should not be indebted to the public, that foreigners should sign a declaration of integration and active citizenship, and that foreigners have passed the test in Danish 2. Some of the consultation responses received from the consultation parties express concern that the bill affects highly qualified foreigners, which companies, educational institutions and authorities otherwise work strategically to recruit and also maintain in Denmark. It is now a requirement to obtain permanent residence permits that foreigners meet at least two out of four additional integration-relevant conditions. The four conditions are, firstly, a requirement of passed citizenship test or active citizenship; secondly, a stricter employment requirement; thirdly, an income requirement; and, fourthly, a stricter language requirement. If all four additional conditions are met, the alien may obtain a permanent residence permit after four years. The bill does not affect this particular model. It will therefore continue to be the case that foreigners who have shown a special ability and willingness to integrate into Danish society can obtain permanent residence permits at an earlier stage. In addition, some of the responses received expressed concern that the bill would mean that an even larger group of the most vulnerable refugees would be excluded from obtaining permanent residence permits. It is the government's view that all resident foreigners should have shown the ability and willingness to integrate into Danish society in order to obtain permanent residence permits. The government wants to encourage indigenous refugees to learn the Danish language and also to get into employment. I believe that the higher demands we make on all foreigners, including refugees, will lead to extra efforts by foreigners to become an integral part of the Danish labour market and society in general. In this way, their opportunities for well-integrated integration will be strengthened. Therefore, refugees should not be allowed to obtain permanent residence permits more easily than all other foreigners, and that is also why, just over a year ago, we removed the particularly lenient access to permanent residence permits for refugees. That remains our position today, I would like to emphasise. Having said that, I would also like to stress that the individual conditions for permanent residence permits in the same way as today will not have to be imposed if Denmark's international obligations, including the UN Convention on Disability, are to do so. The proposed reinforcements therefore do not affect foreigners who are currently exempt from the requirements of Denmark's international obligations. In some of the consultation responses received, the consultation parties have pointed out that the rules on permanent residence permits have changed many times over the last few years. This was also one of the points raised by the Socialist rapporteur and that the entry into force rules mean that the new rules will have retroactive effect. It points out that it creates uncertainty and unpredictability as to the conditions for permanent residence and that it makes it difficult for those concerned to organise their lives. I would therefore like to stress once again that it is basically the government's view that the possibility of obtaining permanent residence permits is a privilege. Therefore, it is also an area where we can, but should also make high demands on the ability and willingness of foreigners to integrate into Danish society. It is with this aim in mind that we are tightening up the rules on permanent residence. In conclusion, I would like to thank you for the support that has been given to this proposal. I look forward very much to the further consideration and the issues that may arise either here today or during the committee's reading.

So it is not because asylum seekers do not come up against Scandinavia, they just do not seek against Denmark on the same scale as before. However, precisely because the situation can change so quickly, we need to have a tool that we can tackle if the situation should go and develop to ensure that there is again a great deal of pressure on the Danish borders. With this bill, the government is proposing that we introduce, as it were, an emergency brake which can be activated if the Minister considers that the Dublin system has actually collapsed. This means that, in exceptional cases, the Danish authorities will be allowed to refuse asylum seekers on entry from another Dublin country as long as the situation makes sense to be able to do so, and it is a very sensible measure to protect Denmark. For I honestly believe that there are a great many Danes who, especially since there was pressure on the Danish borders, simply cannot understand why, if a fugitive comes up through Europe, who migrates from a safe country to a safe country, we should not be able to reject that person at the Danish border, especially when there are many. There we now have a tool that can be used to control the situation if it should arise. That is why we in the Left are very, very happy that there are responsible parties in the Danish Parliament who take this challenge seriously and who support our legislative proposal and therefore, of course, the Left will vote in favour of the bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20170323144955	When Erdogan opened its borders in August-September 2015 to push EU countries, it happened that we experienced huge refugee pressure. We saw refugees on motorways heading not only towards Denmark, but also towards Norway and Sweden, and this called for new solutions. Because what could we do? Refugees came from Germany, a safe country, but they wanted to move on to better countries in Scandinavia. It was to be formally governed by the Dublin Regulation, but in practice it had collapsed at that time. The incentive structure is completely black. If a fugitive has crossed the border and you would like to avoid spending money on processing, just let the person pass on to the next country. This logic benefits both refugees and the border country, which escapes proceedings, but it does not benefit Denmark or Sweden at all. An excessive proportion of the foreigners who came to Denmark in the autumn of 2015 had travelled through a number of Schengen countries without being registered. In autumn 2015, therefore, we learned that the Dublin Regulation is far too dependent on the other Schengen countries to shoulder their responsibilities. It was expensive training, and we see no reason to pay them again. As you know, it is not possible today to reject a person at the border if the person who comes is seeking asylum, even if the person comes from a safe country such as Germany or other Schengen countries, who have failed to cooperate by failing to register him or, moreover, does not fit well at Schengen's extreme borders. We also risked that there were some who infiltrated refugee streams such as Islamists, Assadagenter, Iran agents, Hizbollahagenter. This really was an untenable situation. With this bill, we're giving the government the opportunity to solve the problem that we didn't have the opportunity to solve in autumn 2015. The Refugee Board must provide asylum-related expertise on the question of whether a Dublin country is equivalent to a safe third country and, in the event of a crisis, we can then reject the asylum seeker at the border. It is a question of being ready if similar situations arise again. It is a matter of due diligence. Although this government has actually managed to control the number of refugees, changes can quickly take place. That is why we must also constantly ensure that we have the right means to look after Denmark and, at the same time, to promote freedoms throughout the world, so that we give the weakest part of the world the courage to fight for democracy. The Conservative People's Party supports the bill.
ParlaMint-DK_20170323145306	Thank you, sir. Naser Khader. This did not give rise to any brief comments, which is why we can now welcome the Minister for Foreign Affairs and Integration on the platform.
ParlaMint-DK_20170323145327	Thank you. And thank you for receiving the bill, which has, of course, given rise to some debate. This bill is about being prepared in case the pressure on Denmark increases dramatically. It is simply a matter of showing due care. Now we are seeing very low entry and asylum applicants in Denmark. The number of asylum seekers in January was thus the lowest in almost eight years, with only 223 applicants. The figure shows that government policy works; that the 50 restrictions in the field of foreigners, together with among others. border controls and also measures at EU level have had a significant impact. However, experience also shows that in a very short time there may be a new acute situation that we need to be ready to deal with. Just look at the current situation in Turkey or the worrying entry figures into the European Union via Libya and the so-called Central Mediterranean route. After all, we all remember the dramatic scenes that spawned in autumn 2015, when thousands of refugees and migrants crossed the Danish border and wandered along our motorways towards Norway and, to a large extent, Sweden. The government will ensure that Denmark will not again find itself in a similar situation. The bill will therefore introduce a legal basis into the Aliens Act which will allow asylum seekers to be refused on entry, i.e. immediately at the border, if a crisis situation arises which puts Danish borders under pressure. After all, it is not possible today to reject a foreigner on the border with another country covered by the Dublin Regulation if he applies for asylum. This means that asylum seekers can stay in this country while the National Immigration Authority assesses which Dublin country is responsible for examining the asylum application. If another Dublin country is responsible for examining the application, yes, the alien will, as a rule, be transferred to that country. The Dublin system is based on the fulfilment by all Dublin countries of their obligations. This means, among other things, that the countries must register the foreigners correctly, respond to requests for takeover and cooperation on the transfer itself. If more Dublin countries fail to fulfil their obligations, then it can put pressure on the individual Dublin countries' asylum systems and also on the whole Dublin system. For the government, it is therefore crucial that the authorities have tools that match the challenges we can face if there is a crisis situation in which Dublin cooperation really breaks down. The Government therefore proposes that in the Aliens Act a legal basis should be inserted, after which the Minister for Foreign Affairs and Integration may at any time decide that asylum seekers may be refused on entry from another Dublin country. A prerequisite for the activation of the emergency brake system will be that Dublin cooperation has, in the view of the Danish Government, actually ceased to function. In such a situation, Denmark would no longer be obliged, on the basis of an assessment of international law, to follow Dublin procedures. It will depend on several factors whether the cooperation has actually broken down and will depend, by its very nature, on a concrete assessment of the overall circumstances. A decision to activate the emergency brake will be taken after the proposal for a period of 4 weeks and may then be extended by a further four weeks at a time, and the decision will of course be repealed when the conditions are no longer met. Let me finally make it clear that even if the emergency brake is activated, of course Denmark will still have to comply with its international obligations. This means, among other things, that there may be special cases in which we, for example, have to deal with one another. because of the children's convention rules will be obliged to allow an asylum seeker to enter the country. In conclusion, I would like to thank the rapporteurs who support the proposal here, and I look forward to the further debate. I also have the feeling that this could give rise to questions through the committee, and I am, of course, prepared to answer them.

ParlaMint-DK_20170323151035	Thank you. It is an emergency brake which, together with the government's other 50 tightenings, must ensure that the authorities have the right tools to deal with a situation where the pressure on Denmark is increasing dramatically. It is an emergency brake that is about taking due care. In the light of the previous debate on the emergency brake, I can of course understand that it can be both easy and of course also tempting to go further and generally reject asylum seekers on the border, even if this is not a crisis situation in which Dublin cooperation has collapsed. But it's not that easy. Indeed, it will not be possible to implement the proposal's content on rejection at the border and at the same time remain part of Dublin cooperation. It is therefore basically about whether it is wise for Denmark to break with the Dublin system at this time, and I simply do not think it is. The government is therefore working hard to maintain Denmark's participation in the current Dublin system, but at the same time we are firm that asylum and immigration policy must, of course, be established in Denmark and not in Brussels. At the same time, the government sees it as a basic premise that Denmark is complying with its international obligations and therefore the government cannot support the resolution. Denmark's geographical location means that most asylum seekers' first country of asylum will rarely be Denmark, and we actually benefit from the common EU system. The Dublin Agreement allows us to send a larger number of asylum seekers back every year to countries other than Denmark. In the latest inventory covering 2015, we agreed with other Dublin countries to send 1,607 asylum seekers back to another EU country, while 818 went the other way. We therefore believe that Denmark has an interest in making Dublin cooperation work better and we fear that the adoption of this proposal will be viewed with good reason by the other European countries as a step away from Dublin cooperation, simply because the proposers want to reject asylum seekers on the grounds that they come from a safe country. That is why I am surprised at the motion for a resolution. It must be borne in mind that the main argument behind the Dublin Agreement was precisely to avoid such asylum shopping between European countries, and is that not what we can fear is developing even more if Denmark is not still included in the agreement? It is true that the Dublin system is challenged and, of course, there is no point in individual countries failing to register asylum seekers and simply waving them up through Europe. However, Dublin is still in operation and, in the view of the Social Democrats, the government must work to ensure that Denmark can remain in the new Dublin cooperation without dragging a European quota system over its head. Finally, we in the Social Democratic Party have been involved in tightening up Danish legislation on foreigners. We believe that asylum seekers can and must be rejected at the border if it is found that they are travelling from a safe country, such as Germany. Such a practice would not be contrary to the UN Convention on Refugees, as we know there are a number of politicians and parties who care about it in here. This follows, for example, from the 1951 Refugee Convention, Article 33 (1). 1, prohibiting expulsion or refusal, that no Contracting State may in any way expel or reject a refugee at the borders of such territories where his life or freedom will be threatened by reason of his race, religion, nationality, membership of a special social group or political views. Denmark can therefore, as a clear general rule, reject asylum seekers at the Danish-German border or at other border crossings to Denmark on the grounds that the asylum seeker enters from a safe country. This will be the case, for example, if you enter Denmark from Germany. If the motion for a resolution is adopted if it is adopted at some point, the determination of whether the asylum seeker enters a safe third country must be carried out at or close to the Danish borders, so that the asylum seeker can be repatriated from the country without undue delay. In the Danish People's Party we believe that the most optimal arrangement would be for Denmark to reject asylum seekers and migrants at the border, that is to say, a regular asylum stop, and that we will then take 500 UN quota refugees next year and no more, because in this way we will reduce immigration significantly, and we will be able to control developments here in the Folketing ourselves. I think this is the most honest way of conducting foreign policy. Then those parties who believe that we should take 10,000 a year or 20,000 a year can argue for it in an election campaign, and then they can see how many votes they get for it, and if they get the majority, then they get the majority, and then other parties, it may be a party like the Danish People's Party, can say that we believe that the limit has been reached, that we do not think we can take more this year while next year, we assess, can take 500 UN quota refugees, including families, and that is it. So the Danes know what they have to do with, and frankly that would be the most honest and actually the most democratic thing, because the best thing is for a country to manage the development and the influx itself. This should be the case, but unfortunately it is not the case because a majority in the Danish Parliament rejects the motion for a resolution by the Danish People's Party. We are sorry that we are going to put it again at some point in one version or another, but we have come a bit today with the previous proposal, which has been dealt with, where it has been agreed, as part of the financial agreement between the Danish People's Party and the government, that in particular situations at least, asylum seekers should be rejected at the border. It is, of course, very good that there will now be this opportunity in Danish legislation, but we would like to build on and ensure that all day, all days, all year round, can be denied asylum seekers at the Danish borders. This is clearly in Danish interest in our view.

ParlaMint-DK_20170323162416	Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no more brief comments. We move on in the rapporteur's line to Mrs Ulla Sandbæk from the Alternative.
ParlaMint-DK_20170323162424	Thank you. One of the absolute basic values of the church is to be spacious. In fact, there has been talk of the spacious church since the religious awakenings of the 1800s. The challenge at that time was to ensure that, for example, both the inner mission and the Grundtvigians could feel at home in a common church. This led to the laws of liberty on parish bonding in 1855 and the right to form electoral congregations based on at least 20 households in 1868. In both cases the thinking was that the different believing Christians could leave the local parish either as individuals or as a group, but still remain a part of the church. Since then, the challenges to the spaciousness of the church have changed. The diversity to be accommodated is no longer just about theology. The church is today in a religious market where it can only maintain its position as the church of the great majority if it can relate to the fairly diverse expectations of its members. The task as a national church is therefore to reflect in a religiously responsible way the complexity that exists first and foremost among the members. In this respect, the first task is to realise at all what this complexity looks like. This includes, on the one hand, developing a language for diversity and, on the other hand, conducting membership studies which can give an impression of how widely the various forms of belief and life are, as is relevant to the church. The draft law we are discussing today is based precisely on an investigation, since the Ministry of Church, in collaboration with the National Association of Church Councils, the bishops, the founders and the Danish Provence Association since 2014, has been working to clarify how the framework for the congregation councils' task solution and organisation can be simplified, debureaucratised and made more flexible. The purpose of the bill is, in the Minister's words, to give congregational councils additional space to organise themselves in the way that suits them best. They must have the opportunity to be congregational councils in their way. As you know, the church councils are made up of volunteers and as such reflect the same diversity that we find among the members of the church. Therefore, it makes very good sense that the congregation councils are given the opportunity, on the one hand, to deal with the areas in which they have an interest and, on the other hand, to organise themselves individually as a congregation council and as individual members, and, on the other hand, to have a more flexible framework for the organisation of their tasks so as to facilitate their work. The fact that in future there will be the possibility of creating direct legal bases, where there is now only scope for exemption, will certainly be one of the ways in which the work of the Councils of Congregations can be facilitated, to mention just one example of the range of measures that the bill allows to be introduced. I have taken note of the fact that the hearings have been read and included in the draft law. This is another very positive feature of this bill. In the alternative, we have an idea that more people know more, and the alternative also always looks with great sympathy at as many levels of freedom as possible. So we can be happy to vote in favour of the bill. Thank you.

We saw an example of this when the President's entry ban decree was stopped. In addition to the nuance differences, of course, I can also see discrepancies. They exist, and they also exist in terms of value, between Denmark's policy and some of President Trump's statements. For this discussion is primarily about Denmark's overall relationship with the United States. The government will continue to seek close cooperation with the new administration. We will take this into account in the ongoing formulation of Danish foreign and security policy, and the government will do so on the basis that the United States is a very close ally. So that is what, you might say, will be the point of departure for our work in relation to the new American administration.

That sounds magnificent, you might say, and I actually think it is. I dare not speculate where Denmark would have been in 2017, if after the end of World War II we had not become part of the free Western world with the United States in front. And that I can stand here today on Parliament's platform and speak openly and frankly about our close allies, including those areas where we do not necessarily agree, say much about the social image we have been and are an active part of together with the United States. It is not difficult to see areas where the new American administration, headed by President Trump, and we look at things differently. Among other things, it has. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, we are talking about worrying signals and decisions in relation to the entry ban, free trade, women's rights and also the climate. There are also examples that we were not entirely in line with the previous administration, so in this way there is nothing new just think about Guantánamo, for example, and about women's rights. We are and must not necessarily agree on everything. I think that has always been a condition, and this has not been the case in the past, it can be said that fundamental Danish exchange rate changes have been made every time American decisions have been taken, with which we have not agreed. And that is how I think it should continue to be. We need to hold on to our line. Nor is my expectation that in future Denmark will agree with the United States on everything, but it is clear that the change of President this time will probably bring about greater changes than we have been used to in the past. At least that's the expectation in many places. On the part of the government, we are already well in the process of establishing contacts with the US administration, which are crucial in our efforts to continue close and confidential transatlantic cooperation. I landed this morning from Washington, where I had meetings with senators, with John McCain, and I also had the opportunity to briefly exchange words with both Secretary of Defense Mattis and Secretary of State Tillerson. Although we are approx. Two months in the lifetime of the new administration, it is not possible to say that it is one hundred per cent in place. It is far from being the case with all the political appointments. There's nothing new about that either. Typically, it takes the new US President six months before the new administration is more or less consolidated, and it must also be expected to be the case with Trump if it does not take even longer. The fact that there are not yet people in all important places is, of course, of some importance for the dialogue with partners, but it is also important for the administration's own policy formulation and implementation. I have therefore also emphasized since the elections in my statements, both here in Parliament and in public, that we must also arm ourselves with some patience. We are still at an early stage in the life of the new administration, while with Trump there is a new way of communicating policy. No one is in doubt about that either. Moreover, I think that is something that we just have to be prepared for and to which we will get used to. But I also believe that we must be careful that we do not react to too inadequate a basis. On the other hand, of course, we cannot just wait for the appointments of over 4 000 politically appointed people who will be in the new administration. We work hard at the Ministry of Foreign Affairs every day to understand the full scope of the decisions taken and communicated in the United States. We do so at all levels and also with the involvement of Danish embassies in other countries, from which we obtain relevant analyses and input. We therefore await the actual formulation of the policy before we respond and we do not respond to all statements and tweets. For example, it has proved useful in relation to Trump's view of NATO and the American security guarantee, which has been the basis of peace and freedom in Europe since the end of the Second World War. We are all aware of Trump's statements in the election campaign, which questioned the security guarantee, just as he claimed that NATO was obsolete. Subsequently, both the President, but also Vice-President Pence and Minister for Defence Mattis, have expressed strong support for the alliance. At the same time, however, I note that it is important to get on board and that is the expectations from the United States are unmistakable. NATO member states must increase defence budgets and show political will to meet the commitments made by the Heads of State or Government at the NATO summit in Wales in 2014. This is a problem that will hardly go away. NATO remains at the heart of Danish security policy. It is therefore essential that the transatlantic relationship remains strong and solid. I therefore very much welcome the clear support of the US administration for NATO and, at the same time, look forward to the forthcoming discussions within NATO on the future of the alliance. I also had the opportunity yesterday to briefly exchange words with the NATO Secretary-General, Mr Stoltenberg, who was also recently visiting Copenhagen. There could also be mention of relations with Russia and the situation in Ukraine, where earlier statements have subsequently been made, it has to be said, very nuanced, and where key people such as Vice-President Pence and Foreign Minister Tillerson have brought greater clarity to American policy. On several occasions, we have seen statements from the new administration, which are similar to those of the Obama administration. I continue to believe that it is too early to conclude what Russia's policy will actually be like with Trump at the helm. We are, of course, following this very closely, and we are still working for a line of robustness and principle. We are trying to put the pressure that we can do in relation to that being the line that is being pursued. This is best ensured, not least by the fact that we have a common EU line, with which Trump must also deal. It is important that the European Union continues to reach out to the new US government to seek to continue its cooperation in the area of sanctions policy. It is very much in Denmark's interest that we stand guard over a rule-based international system where gross violations meet with a clear response. At the same time, as I said before, we must also bear in mind that there are many historical examples of cases where we have not always agreed with the United States on the line taken. Guantánamo is an obvious example. We also have such cases with the new American administration, which should not be concealed. There are, for example: some of the different decrees that have been about entry bans and the course around them. It's been a messy affair. Let me begin by making a few comments on the actual process of the entry ban. I immediately described the resolution as unwise and unfair, referring to the fact that we must judge and meet each other as individuals and that the resolution will have very severe consequences for a large number of people and, as a tool for combating terrorism, the resolution was, in my view, too broad in scope. But I also have to stress that, of course, it is the United States itself that decides who they will allow to enter. That's the way it is. As you know, the new entry ban decree is currently in force. We are being put out of hand and, of course, we are following developments closely. The process around the entry ban in a way also illustrates the point of showing patience and not reacting too strongly. Since it was clear that Danish citizens with dual nationality would also be subject to the entry ban, we contacted the US authorities. The reactions from Denmark and other countries concerned led within a few days to the decision by the American team that Danes and other nationalities not subject to the entry ban would not be covered by the decree as long as they traveled on a passport not covered by the ban. It has been a rather unsightly process. I do not think anyone is in any doubt as to where a far-reaching decision has been taken without prior information to other countries about the consequences. The American administration can rightly be criticised for this, and, as you know, the government has done so. Trade policy is an area in which we still have to wait for the new American administration to settle down more precisely. It is clear that right now TTIP has been paused, but unlike the US's major free trade agreement with Asia, the Pacific region, the TPP that Trump has withdrawn from the United States, it is very possible that discussions on strengthening the transatlantic economic partnership will be resumed at some point, and we will look forward to that. Let me also mention women's health and rights, which is an area in which we in Denmark have made a significant commitment to dialogue with the Americans and the international community. Trump's announcement of the re-establishment of the so-called global gag rule has been rejected by the Danish side, as it intends to remove all American support to international organisations that advise, support or actively promote abortion. Denmark is working with like-minded countries to maintain a strong voice for the right of girls and women to decide their own body, which also includes access to information, contraception and safe abortion. With global gag rule, the new US administration has taken a position that we do not agree with. However, as I mentioned earlier, the transatlantic relationship is characterised by the fact that there is also room for us to disagree with each other, and that is the case here. The Minister for Development, together with European colleagues, has taken the lead in ensuring even greater EU focus and support for this important agenda. In addition, on 2 March, Denmark, together with Belgium, Sweden and the Netherlands, hosted an international conference in Brussels aimed at mobilising political and financial support for the agenda. There are, of course, many examples of areas where we have common interests and objectives and where we work closely together. Let me mention the coalition work in the fight against ISIL. The fight against terrorism is at the heart of Danish foreign and security policy, and it was also in this context that I visited Washington yesterday, where we had a major conference in the coalition. Today's greatest threat on this front is ISIL, and the fight against ISIL is taking place in a broad international coalition, which consists of over 60 countries, including, of course, the United States as a central coalition partner. Denmark is one of the first nine countries to be asked about participating in the coalition, and of course we were ready to participate in the work. It is important that we maintain the line. This is a good coalition work and an extremely important agenda, and it is a cooperation in which, of course, we on the part of the government will endeavour to continue and where we have also won significant military victories in recent times. ISIL is retreating. They do not have near the attractiveness they have had in the past, for example. In relation to foreign warriors. In conclusion, I would like to emphasise that Denmark, the European Union and our neighbouring countries will continue to have a large share of interests with the United States on a wide range of global challenges. The global security situation with Russia as well as migration and the fight against terrorism underline the need for cross-atlantic cohesion. We need strong transatlantic cooperation both within and outside NATO in view of the many global challenges we face. The choice of Trump has not changed that, and as long as we do not overreact to individual statements and policies that have not yet been formulated, but seek cooperation and actively work for Denmark's foreign and security policy interests, I am convinced that the transatlantic relationship will continue to ensure peace, stability and growth. And in every meeting I had in the last 24 hours in Washington, that was the signal that was sent clearly by everyone I had conversations with. Americans also attach enormous importance to the continuation of this cooperation, and this must be done in a good spirit. Thank you for the floor.

The relationship between the United States and Europe is unique and strong. It has deep roots in many families, in our culture, in our values and in our history. It is a bond bound together by democratic societies which, for generations and in war and peace, have been close allies and friends. The transatlantic relationship has been deeply anchored in our mutual belief that Europe will become weaker and more uncertain without the United States, that the United States will become weaker and more uncertain without Europe, that NATO will contribute to our common security, and that the alliance will be in our mutual interest, that the European Union will help to strengthen and secure and develop a Europe martret of world wars and the crimes of the Iron Curtain, and that, therefore, a well-functioning European Union will not only be in the interests of Europe but also to a great extent in the interests of the United States. The transatlantic relationship is built on mutual respect and it has been crucial in ensuring peace, stability and growth on both sides of the ocean. This has been the starting point for all the most recent administrations, whether the President has been called Kennedy, Reagan, Clinton, Bush or Obama. That is why it has been a cause for concern that presidential candidate Trump on several occasions rhetorically questioned NATO cooperation, the importance of the European Union, the role of the United Nations, a strong and consistent approach to Russia, free trade and the fight against climate change. The vital question now is: what is rhetoric and what is real policy? Here the picture is not yet clear, because we have seen both positive and highly worrying steps by the leaders of the new government. On the plus side, for example, both Vice-President Pence, Foreign Minister Tillerson and Minister for Defence Mattis have clearly and unequivocally underlined the full participation of the United States in NATO cooperation, with a strong desire for stronger European commitment, now, but also so crucially, with unmistakeable statements that NATO is not an outdated institution, but rather the very cornerstone of the transatlantic security structure, with all that it entails, including the muskete nest in Article 5. This is extremely important, not least at a time when we are seeing an increasingly aggressive Russia that has annexed Crimea and which is rattling with the sabre in relation to the Baltic States and the Baltic Sea. In such a situation, there is no room for NATO countries to go their separate ways. We must stand together so that Putin and Russia cannot exploit differences and divisions across the Atlantic. Unfortunately, the new US administration has also offered serious disappointments, and only last week we saw how the American Finance Minister, in the context of the Baden-Baden G20 meeting in Germany, blocked the adoption of the crucial and quite usual declaration on the importance of the fight against protectionism and climate change. There is no reason to hide the fact that this was an extremely worrying step in the wrong direction, and from the point of view of Denmark and Europe it is important that this signal be sent to the President of the United States and his people, because here it is about growth, jobs and the future of our planet, and here we need a United States which, together with Europe, is at the forefront, and not a United States which chooses to be a stop-block. For social democracy, the transatlantic relationship remains of the utmost importance. We believe that Europe is dependent on the United States, but also that the United States is dependent on Europe when it comes to our common security, and the dangers are many, and there are very dark thunder clouds in the sky. We are seeing a more aggressive Russia, a Turkey where the situation is becoming more serious day by day, a peace process between Israel and the Palestinians, which is in deep crisis, a Syria on fire and an important fight against the terrorism of the Islamic State. We are experiencing an unstable North Africa and a North Korea that continues to move in an increasingly worrying direction. The global reality therefore tells us not less, but more cooperation between Europe and the United States, and it would be an historic mistake if the United States chose to withdraw from the world rather than try to influence it. Even the United States cannot stand alone and the international community needs the joint progressive efforts of the United States and the European Union if the world is to become a really better place to live. At the same time, however, I believe that we must realistically expect that in a number of areas there will be greater differences in political positions than we have traditionally been used to in relations between the United States and Europe. This applies especially to free trade, climate policy and the importance of a strong UN. Here it is crucial that Denmark and the European Union do what we can to move the United States in a progressive direction and that we do not hide from our American friends that we are dealing here with vital Danish and European interests, that we in Europe look forward to cooperation with the new US Government, but that we will continue to fight for what we believe in, even when it means that we disagree with the President of the White House. Denmark's message to President Trump should therefore be that America must not mean cooperation with Europe until the end, that we will work together and that it is in mutual interest. I shall now read out a proposal for adoption. The Folketing supports the government's efforts to maintain a close transatlantic relationship based on the many common interests and values. The Danish Parliament looks with concern at the announcements made at, among others. free trade and climate change, but at the same time notes that the Trump administration has not fundamentally called into question the importance of close transatlantic relations. The Danish Parliament supports the government's ongoing position on the new administration's foreign and security policy as it is formulated. ParlaMint-DK_20170323180234	Thank you. The Americans are our friends. There is no doubt that we are going through some rather hectic years on the foreign policy stage.
