Closer relations between the West and Russia should ensure lasting stability through increased international cooperation. But it wasn't supposed to be that way. The annexation of Crimea and Russia's self-understanding as regards a Russian sphere of interest between Russia and Europe makes it, to say the least, difficult. Our partners in the European Union do so, and they do so in the United States. The United States is often referred to as one of Denmark's closest allies, but the relationship between our two countries is even deeper than only common interests on the foreign policy stage. I believe that common fundamental values are the fundamentals of our mutual relationship. When The Founding Fathers issued the American Declaration of Independence on 4 July 1776 they formulated a number of principles which later became the foundation of the constitutions of many Western countries, including the Danish Constitution. The Declaration of Independence confirmed that we as human beings were born equal and that each has inviolable rights, including the right to life itself, to freedom and to seek happiness. A few years later, in the context of the French Revolution of 1789, the same principles were reiterated in the French Declaration of Human Rights. The two events have been decisive steps on the road to the modern enlightened idea of human innate rights. The seeds of liberal democracy, as we know it today, were thus added, and even though history has since the second half of the 18th century offered deviations from the democratic right-based course, it is the fundamental values that form the Danes' spiritual community with the Americans. As both the Prime Minister and the Foreign Minister have pointed out, Denmark must, of course, follow the course of President Trump's administration and the barn, and there have been decisions and announcements which have given rise to a further sinking. The administration is still new and, instead of fearing the worst, we must hope for the best and, at the very least, arm ourselves with patience and calm in our stomachs. We must stand by our positions and recognise that we do not have to agree on everything, especially when we agree on the fundamentals. We have stood side by side with the Americans against the Nazis, against the Communists during the Cold War and now against the terrorist threat of the Islamists. The United States is an extremely important ally, which is why it is also good that today in the Folketing we have the opportunity to express our views on transatlantic cooperation. If it is to the Liberal Alliance, a continued strong cooperation for peace, for security, for freedom and for free trade is essential, and although statements, and especially tweets from the new US President, sometimes give rise to raised eyebrows, it does not change the fundamental and historically anchored community of values that we have with the Americans.

That is to say, we are actually, in our opinion, in a very, very serious situation, and I think that we need to act in a slightly stronger way than what the majority is doing in the proposal for adoption that has been presented here today and in the approach that we have. Adoption cannot define a foreign policy, but it reflects an approach to this problem. So we actually believe that we need to have a slightly more critical approach to the President of the United States. Let me make a brief historical review back to 2001 when Anders Fogh Rasmussen became Prime Minister and actually carried out what I would call a paradigm change in Danish foreign policy. Where one had previously been very, very European-oriented, Anders Fogh Rasmussen came to, and with the Iraq war in particular he had a clear ambition that together with Britain we should be the most pro-American country in Europe. This is what Rumsfeld called the new Europe, unlike the majority of the countries of the rest of Europe, the EU, which Rumsfeld called the old Europe. It was, in fact, a new orientation that was never really discussed at the time, but which has meant that Denmark came to lie extremely close to the United States with virtually everything. This was especially true of Anders Fogh Rasmussen, and he also received a reward for it, so to speak, but still it was an extremely close approach to the United States. The question is whether it is still wise to have the approach that the question is whether we do not have to ask ourselves sometimes whether we cannot have any other interests than to be as close to the United States as we did then and as we do. Is it necessary for us to be the country of the European Union which, after Britain has resigned, is the country closest to Washington? Can there not sometimes be any need for a critical approach in which we do something other than what the United States wants, it cannot be in our interest perhaps to seek against some other allies than Washington, elsewhere to define its policy in? I think so. In particular, it applies to the European Union, the European line. I myself think it is a bit important to be more European, to spend more effort on the EU's being able to play a stronger role, because I fear that there will be a vacuum if the United States takes serious steps to withdraw from international cooperation and that it may then be China or other powers that come in instead. I believe that the European Union needs to go in and try to fill such a vacuum, and that is why we think it is important. On behalf of the SF, I should like to make the following points: Proposals for adoption The Danish Parliament supports the work for a continued close transatlantic relationship based on the many interests and values shared. The Danish Parliament notes, however, that the newly elected President of the United States has made statements and views which must give rise to deep concern. This applies not least to his reports on the EU, NATO, free trade, climate change, the Middle East, US armaments, discrimination in immigration policy, abortion, etc. The Folketing urges the government to formulate a foreign policy with a strong EU perspective that has a broader aim than the close relationship with the United States. This means, among other things, that Denmark must V 55).
